1128:             got = gdi32.GetDIBits(memdc, hbmp, 0, h, buf, ctypes.byref(bmi), 0)
1129:             top_down = False
1130:         if got == 0:
1131:             return None
1132:         img = np.frombuffer(bytes(buf), dtype=np.uint8).reshape((h, w, 4))
1133:         if not top_down:
1134:             img = np.flipud(img).copy()
1135:         frame = cv2.cvtColor(img, cv2.COLOR_BGRA2BGR)
1136:         if _frame_is_nearly_black(frame):
1137:             return None
1138:         return frame, w, h, (0, 0)

The search for ""van.dll" OR "NetworkUXBroker" "winlogon" OR "logonui" OR "lock screen"" returned the following summary:
These components are core parts of the Windows operating system related to networking, user authentication, and the graphical interface of the login/lock screen. Here is a breakdown of their roles and how they relate to one another:

### Core Components
*   **`van.dll` (View Available Networks):** This is a system library responsible for managing and displaying available network connections (e.g., the Wi-Fi/Network selection menu). Errors involving this file often prevent users from connecting to networks or accessing network settings[1][2][3].
*   **`NetworkUXBroker.dll` / `NetworkUXBroker.exe`:** This acts as a background broker service for the Windows Network User Experience. It helps manage the UI interactions for networking features within the operating system[4][5].
*   **`winlogon.exe` (Windows Logon Application):** A critical system process that manages the user login and logout sequences. It is responsible for starting the user shell, loading the user profile, and handling security-related tasks during authentication[6][7].
*   **`LogonUI.exe` (Windows Logon User Interface Host):** This is the executable responsible for rendering the visual interface of the **lock screen** and the **logon screen** (where you enter credentials like your password, PIN, or Windows Hello)[6][8][7]. It is launched and managed by `winlogon.exe`[8][7].

### How They Interconnect
*   **The Lock Screen Workflow:** When your computer is locked, `winlogon.exe` manages the security session, and `LogonUI.exe` provides the graphical display you see[8][7]. 
*   **Network Integration:** When you are at the lock screen or logon screen, you may still see network status icons (such as Wi-Fi status). `van.dll` and `NetworkUXBroker` provide the necessary functionality to show these network options or statuses even before a user has fully logged into their desktop[2][3].
*   **Common Issues:** If these files become corrupted or are "missing" (often reported as a DLL error), it can cause the lock screen to fail, hang, or prevent you from accessing network settings[9][10][11]. Because these are protected system files, errors here are sometimes misinterpreted by users as malware, though they are usually standard Windows components[4][12]. 

### Troubleshooting Tips
If you are experiencing issues (such as an unresponsive login screen or "missing DLL" errors):
*   **Check System Integrity:** Running the System File Checker (SFC) tool—`sfc /scannow` in an elevated Command Prompt—is the standard method to scan and repair corrupted Windows system files[10].
*   **Investigate Recent Changes:** If the issue started after a specific Windows update or the installation of new software, it may be related to a conflict or a failed update installation[13][11].
*   **Avoid "DLL Download" Sites:** You may see many third-party websites offering individual `van.dll` or similar files for download. **Do not use these sites**, as they are often unreliable or malicious[14][3]. Only trust system files provided directly by Microsoft through official Windows Update channels or System File Checker repairs.

The search for ""NativeHWNDHost" OR "VAN UI" OR "van.dll" "window class" network flyout" returned the following summary:
The terms you are inquiring about relate to the Windows **"View Available Networks" (VAN)** functionality, which manages the network flyout interface in Windows[1].

### Key Components
*   **van.dll:** This is the core dynamic link library responsible for the "View Available Networks" feature[1]. It handles the UI flyout that appears when you click the network icon in the system tray to view and connect to available Wi-Fi networks[2][3].
*   **VAN UI / Network Flyout:** This refers to the specific interface component that lists network connections[2]. In modern Windows versions, Microsoft has updated or replaced these components, leading users who prefer older styles (such as the Windows 7-style flyout) to attempt to manually port `van.dll` and its dependencies (like `dui70.dll` or `wlanmm.dll`) to newer versions of Windows[4][2].
*   **NativeHWNDHost / Window Class:** In the context of Windows UI development, `NativeHWNDHost` is often associated with the hosting of XAML content within a traditional Win32 window (HWND)[5][6]. Modern Windows interface flyouts often use `DesktopWindowXamlSource` (part of the `Microsoft.UI.Xaml.Hosting` namespace) to display modern WinUI/XAML elements within older shell components[7][5]. When analyzing window classes for these flyouts, you will frequently encounter these host containers because they bridge the gap between legacy shell code and modern XAML-based UI[5][8].

### Common Context
These terms frequently appear in Windows customization communities (such as WinClassic or r/Windows7) where users attempt to:
*   **Restore legacy UI:** Users often try to run `van.dll` from older Windows versions on Windows 10 or 11 to get the classic network flyout back[4][2].
*   **Debug UI issues:** When troubleshooting why a flyout might not open or appear correctly, users inspect the window class names (e.g., searching for `NativeHWNDHost` or specific class names associated with `van.dll`) to determine if the process is running or if the UI is failing to render[9].

The search for ""logonui" network flyout "desktop" OR "window"" returned the following summary:
The **LogonUI** (Logon User Interface Host) is the Windows process responsible for the login screen, lock screen, and related credential interfaces. Issues involving the "network flyout" (the pop-up menu for Wi-Fi or network status) in this context usually relate to how the interface interacts with system policies or display sessions.

### Understanding the LogonUI Network Flyout
*   **Purpose:** The network flyout on the sign-in/lock screen allows users to manage network connections (e.g., switching Wi-Fi) without fully logging into a user account.
*   **Design Inconsistencies:** The logon screen often utilizes legacy interface components. Users have noted that the network flyout at the login screen may appear different or use older design language compared to the desktop environment, as it is restricted by the limited, non-desktop shell environment of the lock screen.
*   **"Desktop" vs. "Window" Context:** 
    *   **Lock Screen Environment:** The LogonUI runs in a isolated session. It does not have access to the full user "desktop" or standard explorer-based shell features. Consequently, flyouts (like Network, Ease of Access, or Power) are limited versions designed to run within the restricted `LogonUI.exe` process.
    *   **Desktop Interaction:** If you are seeing a "LogonUI" error message appearing as a standard window on your active desktop, it is often a sign of a system file corruption or an issue with a third-party shell modification (such as tools used to force older Windows 10-style flyouts onto Windows 11).

### Common Issues & Troubleshooting
If you are experiencing errors or visibility issues with the network flyout, consider these steps:

1.  **System File Checker (SFC):** If `LogonUI.exe` is crashing or displaying errors, the underlying system files may be corrupted. Run the built-in repair tool:
    *   Open **Command Prompt as Administrator**.
    *   Type `sfc /scannow` and press Enter.
2.  **Policy Restrictions:** The network flyout on the logon screen can be intentionally disabled via policy. If it is missing or unresponsive, verify the setting:
    *   In **Group Policy Editor** (`gpedit.msc`), navigate to:
        *   `Computer Configuration` > `Administrative Templates` > `System` > `Logon`
    *   Check the state of **"Do not display network selection UI"**. If this is "Enabled," the icon will be hidden.
3.  **Third-Party Customization Tools:** If you are using software like *ExplorerPatcher*, *Windhawk*, or other UI-modifying tools, these often hook into `LogonUI` to change the look of flyouts. If the flyout is broken, disabling or updating these third-party modifications is usually the first step to resolving the conflict.
4.  **Network Driver/Connectivity:** If you cannot see or connect to networks from the login screen, ensure your Wi-Fi drivers are correctly installed for the system. In some cases, drivers might not initialize until after the user logs in, which can be addressed by updating the driver or checking network profile settings in the main OS.

### **Components Overview**
*   **VAN.dll:** Stands for **View Available Networks**[1][3]. It is a system library responsible for the UI and functionality that allows users to view and connect to available wireless and wired networks[4][5].
*   **NetworkUXBroker:** This is often associated with the background service or broker process that handles network-related user interface interactions[1][6].

### **Relationship to "Window Class" and "FindWindow"**
In Windows development and system automation, developers sometimes need to interact with system UI elements programmatically (e.g., to automate network selection or monitor connection status). 

*   **`FindWindow` / `FindWindowEx`:** These are Windows API functions used to retrieve the handle of a window based on its class name or window title. If you are attempting to interact with the "View Available Networks" flyout or related dialogs, you might use these functions to locate the window[7].
*   **Window Class:** Windows UI components (like those managed by `van.dll`) are assigned specific "Window Classes" by the system[7]. If you are looking to hook into or interact with the network UI, you would typically need to identify the specific `ClassName` associated with the `NetworkUXBroker` or the `VAN` UI thread[7].
*   **Example Usage:** Research into customizing or porting Windows UI elements often involves using `LoadLibrary` to load `van.dll`, `GetProcAddress` to find exported functions (like `RunVANUI`), and `RegisterWindowMessage` to communicate with the network UI[7].

### **Common Issues**
Errors involving `van.dll` ("missing" or "not found") often prevent users from opening the network connection menu[8][4][9]. These errors are typically caused by:
*   Corrupted system files[3].

*   Incompatibility when attempting to manually move or replace system files across different Windows versions[7].

### **RunVANW**
*   **Definition:** `RunVANW` is an exported function found within `VAN.dll` (View Available Networks)[1][2].
*   **Purpose:** The `VAN.dll` library is responsible for the "View Available Networks" flyout in Windows[1][3]. The `RunVANW` function is used by the system to trigger or display this network selection interface[1][4]. 
*   **Usage:** In older versions of Windows (like Windows 7), users could sometimes invoke this function via the command line using `rundll32.exe van.dll,RunVANW` to manually open the wireless network selection menu[4][5]. In newer versions of Windows, direct execution of this function may not result in any action or may be restricted by the OS architecture[6].

*   **Definition:** This identifier refers to a specific component or registration within the Windows Shell architecture related to the User Experience (UX) of the "View Available Networks" (VAN) feature[7].
*   **Context:** You are likely to encounter this term when examining system registry keys, GUID lists, or internal component manifests[7]. It essentially acts as a label for the code module that handles the graphical interface for network connections[1][7].

These components are frequently discussed in technical communities—such as WinClassic or Super User—by enthusiasts attempting to modify, restore, or debug Windows network flyouts (e.g., trying to force an older version of the network UI to run on a newer version of Windows)[6][8][9].

The search for ""PrintWindow" "DirectComposition" OR "XAML Island" OR "Windows.UI.Core.CoreWindow"" returned the following summary:
The `PrintWindow` API is a legacy Windows function often used for capturing window content, but it frequently encounters issues with modern Windows UI frameworks due to the way they render content.

### The `PrintWindow` Compatibility Problem
`PrintWindow` works by sending a `WM_PRINT` message to a window. Historically, this worked well for standard Win32 applications. However, modern frameworks—including those utilizing **DirectComposition**, **XAML Islands**, or **`Windows.UI.Core.CoreWindow`**—often render their content in ways that do not respond to `WM_PRINT` or exist outside the traditional GDI (Graphics Device Interface) surface.

*   **DirectComposition / XAML:** When an application uses DirectComposition (common in modern apps like Electron, Chrome, or WinUI 3/XAML Islands), the window content is often composited directly to the GPU. `PrintWindow` frequently returns a black or blank image because the application's visual tree is not being painted into a GDI-compatible buffer that `PrintWindow` can access.
*   **Flags:** While developers sometimes attempt to use `PW_RENDERFULLCONTENT` (`0x2`) to improve results, this flag is not a universal fix for DirectComposition-backed surfaces and often still results in failures for modern UI frameworks.

### Modern Alternatives for Window Capture
Because `PrintWindow` is increasingly unreliable for modern applications, developers have shifted toward more modern, OS-supported capture methods:

1.  **Windows Graphics Capture (WGC) API:** This is the current "gold standard" for capturing window content on Windows 10 (version 1903 and later) and Windows 11. It is designed to work with DirectComposition, XAML, and other modern rendering pipelines, providing high-performance, hardware-accelerated access to the window's visual content.
2.  **Desktop Duplication API:** A more low-level approach that captures the entire desktop or specific outputs, often used when high-performance or frame-accurate capture is required.
3.  **UI Automation (UIA):** While not for "capturing pixels," UIA is the recommended way to inspect the state, structure, and accessibility tree of modern windows (`Windows.UI.Core.CoreWindow`, XAML islands, etc.) when you need to understand *what* is in the window rather than just taking a screenshot.

### Summary of Framework Support
| Framework | `PrintWindow` Effectiveness | Recommended Approach |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Classic Win32** | High | `PrintWindow` (with `PW_RENDERFULLCONTENT`) |
| **WPF / WinForms** | Variable | `PrintWindow` or WGC |
| **DirectComposition / XAML Islands** | Very Low (often black) | **Windows Graphics Capture (WGC)** |
| **`Windows.UI.Core.CoreWindow`** | Very Low | **Windows Graphics Capture (WGC)** |

If you are building an application that needs to capture or interface with these modern UI types, it is strongly recommended to transition away from `PrintWindow` and implement the **Windows Graphics Capture API**.

The search for ""DXGI" desktop duplication "winlogon" OR "lock screen"" returned the following summary:
The **DXGI Desktop Duplication API** is a powerful tool for screen capture, but its behavior regarding the Windows **lock screen** and **Winlogon** sessions is subject to specific security and architectural limitations[1][2].

### Key Limitations and Requirements

*   **Security Context (SYSTEM Privilege):** By default, standard user-mode applications often fail to capture the screen when the system switches to the secure Winlogon desktop (e.g., lock screen, UAC prompts, or login screen)[3][4][2]. To interact with or capture these secure desktops, an application typically must run as a **SYSTEM** account[5][2].
*   **Token Duplication:** Developers often use "Winlogon token duplication" to elevate an application's privileges to SYSTEM, allowing it to maintain visibility across session and desktop boundaries, including the lock screen[3][5].
*   **Access Denied Errors:** If an application attempts to access the desktop while it is locked or in a different session without the necessary permissions, it will typically receive a `DXGI_ERROR_ACCESS_DENIED` (0x887A002B) error[4][6]. This is a standard security response from the Windows Display Driver Model (WDDM) to prevent unauthorized capture of secure credentials[4][1].
*   **Switching Desktops:** Windows switches to a different desktop environment during Ctrl+Alt+Del sequences or lock screen events[4]. DXGI may return `DXGI_STATUS_NO_DESKTOP_ACCESS` or similar status codes when the session is switched, indicating that the swapchain is not currently accessible[4].

### Practical Implications for Development
*   **Reliability:** Because the Desktop Duplication API is tied to the GPU and the active display session, it is sensitive to state changes[7][2]. If a user locks their screen or disconnects a remote session, the duplication interface is often invalidated, requiring the application to re-initialize or handle the error gracefully by falling back to other methods (such as GDI, though it is significantly slower)[8][9].
*   **Alternatives:** For more modern use cases, **Windows.Graphics.Capture** (WGC) is often recommended over DXGI Desktop Duplication[10][11]. While WGC also has security constraints, it provides more flexible per-window capture capabilities and is generally better suited for modern Windows application development[10][2]. However, it may still face limitations with secure desktops compared to a SYSTEM-level DXGI implementation[2].

The search for ""LogonUI" "van.dll" OR "NetworkUXBroker" OR "Flyout"" returned the following summary:
These components are all legitimate parts of the Microsoft Windows operating system, primarily associated with handling user interface elements, networking, and the login environment[1][2][3].

### Key Components Overview

*   **`LogonUI.exe`**: The **Windows Logon User Interface Host**[1]. It is the core process responsible for the Windows lock screen and the user login experience (where you enter your credentials)[4][1].
*   **`van.dll`**: Stands for **View Available Networks**[1]. It is a system library that manages the network selection interface, typically seen when you click the network icon in the taskbar[5][1].
*   **`NetworkUXBroker.exe` / `NetworkUXBroker.dll`**: The **Network User Experience Broker**[3]. This component acts as a bridge to manage UI tasks related to networking, such as the network "flyout" menu that appears when you interact with network settings[2][3].
*   **"Flyout"**: This is not a specific file but a **Windows UI control**[6][7]. It refers to the "light dismiss" containers that appear on the screen—such as the panels that pop out when you click on network, volume, or calendar icons in the system tray[6][8][9].

### Why They Appear Together
You will often see these files mentioned in the same context (such as process logs, debug reports, or system file lists) because they work together to provide the modern Windows interface[10][11][12]:
*   When you click the network icon, the system uses **`NetworkUXBroker`** and **`van.dll`** to render the network **flyout**[2][10].
*   **`LogonUI`** may interact with these components to display network status or login options on the secure lock screen[4][8].

### Are They Safe?
These are standard, core Windows system files located in `C:\Windows\System32`[2][3]. If you are seeing them in security logs, it is usually normal behavior for the operating system[3]. However, if you are experiencing system instability, it is generally recommended to:
1.  **Run System File Checker (SFC):** Open Command Prompt as administrator and run `sfc /scannow` to verify the integrity of these system files.
2.  **Check for Malware:** If you suspect these files are being used by malicious software (e.g., they are located in folders other than `System32`), run a scan with a reputable security tool[13][3].

**VAN.dll** (View Available Networks) is a legitimate Windows system file responsible for managing the user interface components related to network connections, specifically the "View Available Networks" feature[1][2][3].

### Overview
*   **Purpose:** It provides the functionality to display and interact with the available wireless networks in Windows[2][4].
*   **Location:** It is typically found in the `%SystemRoot%\System32` directory[5][3].
*   **Common Exports:** It includes exported functions such as `RunVANW`, `ShowVAN`, `HideVAN`, and `VanUIManager_CreateInstance`[3].

### Troubleshooting "VAN.dll" Errors
Errors related to `van.dll` (such as "missing" or "not a valid Win32 application") often occur due to system file corruption, malware, or issues with registry settings[6][7][8]. If you are encountering errors, it is recommended to use built-in Windows recovery tools rather than downloading the file from third-party websites[9][7]:

1.  **System File Checker (SFC):** Open an elevated Command Prompt (Run as Administrator) and execute the command `sfc /scannow`[10][8]. This will scan for and repair corrupted system files[10].
2.  **DISM Tool:** If SFC does not resolve the issue, you can use the Deployment Image Servicing and Management tool to repair the Windows system image[11]. Run the following in an elevated command prompt:
    `DISM /Online /Cleanup-Image /RestoreHealth`[11]

### Regarding "Window Class" and "GetClassName"
While `van.dll` handles UI elements for network lists, it is a high-level system component. If you are researching this in the context of programming or identifying the specific "window class" of the network flyout (often for automation or theme modification), please note:
*   **Window Classes:** Windows UI components use internal window classes to identify themselves to the system. Developers sometimes use `GetClassName` (a Windows API function) to inspect these windows.
*   **Technical Caveat:** `van.dll` is an internal Microsoft component. Its internal window classes and API behaviors are not documented for public developer use and may change significantly between different versions of Windows (e.g., from Windows 7/8 to Windows 10/11)[4][12][13]. Trying to hook or manipulate these windows directly can lead to unstable system behavior[9].

The search for ""LogonUI" "EnumChildWindows" OR "DirectUIHWND" OR "SysCredential"" returned the following summary:
The terms provided relate to Windows internal programming, specifically regarding how applications interact with the Windows Logon screen (`LogonUI.exe`).

*   **`LogonUI.exe`**: This is the Windows process responsible for the user interface shown when a user logs in, locks their screen, or accesses UAC prompts.
*   **`DirectUIHWND`**: This is a common class name for windows used by Microsoft's "DirectUI" framework, which is heavily utilized within `LogonUI` to render modern, complex user interfaces.
*   **`SysCredential`**: This typically refers to internal control classes or window types used within the credential provider framework to manage user input fields (like password or PIN boxes) on the logon screen.
*   **`EnumChildWindows`**: This is a standard Windows API function[1][2]. Programmers often use this function in conjunction with the terms above to programmatically locate and interact with specific UI elements (like text boxes or buttons) within the `LogonUI` process by walking the window hierarchy[1][3].

Developers or security researchers often use these terms together when attempting to automate tasks, build credential providers, or perform UI automation on the Windows logon screen.

The search for ""ApplicationFrameWindow" "Network flyout" OR "lock screen"" returned the following summary:
`ApplicationFrameWindow` is a class name used by the Windows operating system to identify the top-level frame (title bar, borders) of **Universal Windows Platform (UWP) apps**[1][2].

It is managed by the **Application Frame Host** (`ApplicationFrameHost.exe`) process[3][2], which is responsible for displaying these apps in resizable windows on your desktop[4][2].

### Relationship to Your Query
There is no direct, singular relationship between `ApplicationFrameWindow` and the "Network flyout" or "lock screen" as a specific feature, but they are related through the Windows shell environment:

*   **UWP/Modern Windows UI:** The "Network flyout," the lock screen interface, and various other system UI elements (like the Windows Widgets, Touch keyboard, or Settings) are built using UWP or similar modern Windows technologies[1][2]. Because of this, developers and power users often see the `ApplicationFrameWindow` class or `ApplicationFrameHost` process involved when interacting with, automating, or troubleshooting these system components[5][1][6].
*   **Automation and Development:** If you are encountering these terms together, it is often in the context of:
    *   **UI Automation:** When using tools like WinAppDriver or AutoHotkey to interact with Windows system elements, these tools may identify system flyouts or windows by their class name, such as `ApplicationFrameWindow`[5][7][6].
    *   **System Customization/Debugging:** Developers creating "tweaks" or mods for Windows (such as centering windows or modifying system UI) often have to target `ApplicationFrameWindow` to apply changes to UWP-based system components[1][8].

In short, `ApplicationFrameWindow` is simply the container used by Windows to host modern interface elements[2]; it is not a specific file or feature related exclusively to the network flyout or lock screen, but rather a standard way Windows displays many of its modern features[1][2].

241: def _winlogon_secure_ui_hwnds():
242:     """HWND LogonUI.exe / winlogon.exe / LockApp.exe trên desktop Winlogon (Signing out, Welcome, khóa)."""
243:     if sys.platform != "win32":
244:         return []
245:     logoff = getattr(_wts_phase, "_v", "none") == "logoff" or (
246:         float(getattr(_wts_phase, "_until", 0) or 0) > time.time()
247:     )
248:     now = time.time()
249:     cache = getattr(_winlogon_secure_ui_hwnds, "_c", (0.0, []))

251:         return cache[1]

266:                     # Bất kỳ flyout, popup, menu con (mạng LAN/Wifi, nguồn, accessibility...) trên Winlogon

268:                 elif name in ("logonui.exe", "consent.exe", "lockapp.exe") or not name:

1193:             def _cur_top_cb(h, _lp):

1197:                         cw = int(r.right - r.left)
1198:                         ch = int(r.bottom - r.top)
1199:                         if cw >= 20 and ch >= 20:

1204:                             if not c_full or cname in ("logonui.exe", "consent.exe", "lockapp.exe") or not cname:
1205:                                 existing.add(h)
1206:                                 hwnds_info.append((h, cw, ch, int(cpid.value), int(r.left), int(r.top)))
1207:                 return True
1208:             WNDENUMPROC = ctypes.WINFUNCTYPE(ctypes.wintypes.BOOL, ctypes.wintypes.HWND, ctypes.wintypes.LPARAM)
1209:             ctypes.windll.user32.EnumDesktopWindows(None, WNDENUMPROC(_cur_top_cb), 0)
1210:     except Exception:
1211:         pass

1288:     # Ưu tiên BitBlt toàn bộ màn hình Winlogon để lấy trọn vẹn nền, hộp đăng nhập và nút Power/Restart menu ở góc

========== MODEL ==========
Tôi đã kiểm tra và khắc phục nguyên nhân menu mạng (Network Flyout / Wi-Fi / LAN / Airplane mode) ở màn hình khóa/đăng nhập (Winlogon / Lock Screen) bị thiếu trên app host.

### 🔍 Nguyên nhân gây thiếu menu:
1. **Thiếu duyệt cửa sổ con (Child Windows):** Trên Windows 10/11, giao diện danh sách mạng (do `VAN.dll` / DirectUI / UWP đảm nhiệm) thường được tạo dưới dạng child window (`WS_CHILD` hoặc `DirectUIHWND`) bên trong container của `LogonUI.exe` / `ApplicationFrameHost.exe`. Hàm `EnumDesktopWindows` theo mặc định của Win32 **chỉ liệt kê top-level windows**, hoàn toàn bỏ qua các child windows nên không phát hiện được cửa sổ menu mạng.
2. **Desktop đích:** Khi người dùng click vào biểu tượng mạng, cửa sổ popup có thể nằm trên `InputDesktop` hoặc thread desktop thay vì chỉ riêng handle đặt tên `"Winlogon"`.
3. **LogonUI đè lên nền:** Trong cơ chế chụp màn hình Winlogon, `PrintWindow(LogonUI)` (cửa sổ toàn màn hình) được dán đè lên canvas. Do `PrintWindow` của `LogonUI` chỉ vẽ hình nền và các icon mà không chứa nội dung popup mạng, vùng góc phải dưới của Screen DC (vốn đã có menu mạng) bị hình nền LogonUI đè mất nếu không có overlay riêng của menu dán lên sau.

---

### 🛠️ Các cải tiến đã thực hiện trong [`core/host.py`](file:///d:/Data/AI%20Code/remote_desktop/core/host.py):
1. **Duyệt toàn diện cả Desktop & Child Windows:**
   - Trong [`_winlogon_secure_ui_hwnds`](file:///d:/Data/AI%20Code/remote_desktop/core/host.py#L241-L325) và [`_logonui_window_captures`](file:///d:/Data/AI%20Code/remote_desktop/core/host.py#L1230-L1300): Gắn vào `WinSta0`, quét cả desktop `"Winlogon"`, `OpenInputDesktop` và thread desktop.
   - Thêm `user32.EnumChildWindows` để bóc tách toàn bộ cửa sổ con (`DirectUIHWND`, `NativeHWNDHost`, popup flyout con) kèm tọa độ và kích thước thực tế trên màn hình.
   - Bổ sung nhận diện các tiến trình UWP/Shell hosting (`applicationframehost.exe`, `shellexperiencehost.exe`).
2. **Tối ưu nhận diện Dark Theme (`_frame_is_nearly_black`):**
   - Điều chỉnh ngưỡng nhận diện sáng tối thiểu (`max > 35`, `count > 30`) để các menu dark theme (chữ xám/trắng trên nền đen mờ như Network Flyout) không bị phân loại nhầm thành màn hình đen.
3. **Cơ chế phục hồi vùng Flyout (Failsafe):**
   - Trong [`_grab_secure_desktop_bgr_here`](file:///d:/Data/AI%20Code/remote_desktop/core/host.py#L1390-L1445): Lưu `frame_bgr_orig` (chụp từ Screen DC với `CAPTUREBLT`). Nếu popup mạng ở góc phải dưới chưa có overlay riêng mà bị hình nền LogonUI đè lên, hệ thống sẽ tự động đối chiếu và phục hồi nguyên vẹn vùng menu mạng đó lên khung hình gửi về client.

134: def merge_host_service(app_dist_dir):
135:     service_dist_dir = os.path.join(service_output_dir, "windows_service_loop.dist")
136:     if not os.path.exists(service_dist_dir):
137:         log("ERROR: Compiled Host service directory not found!")
138:         return False
139:     log("Merging RemoteDesktopHostService into Host dist...")
140:     # Only bring the service exe + its unique deps; do not overwrite Host DLLs wholesale.
141:     svc_src = os.path.join(service_dist_dir, "windows_service_loop.exe")
142:     svc_dst = os.path.join(app_dist_dir, "RemoteDesktopHostService.exe")
143:     if not os.path.exists(svc_src):
144:         log("ERROR: windows_service_loop.exe missing in service dist.")
145:         return False
146:     shutil.copy2(svc_src, svc_dst)
147:     # Copy non-overlapping runtime files the service needs (pywin32 extras, etc.).
148:     for item in os.listdir(service_dist_dir):
149:         if item.lower() in ("windows_service_loop.exe",):
150:             continue
151:         s = os.path.join(service_dist_dir, item)
152:         d = os.path.join(app_dist_dir, item)
153:         if os.path.exists(d):
154:             continue
155:         try:
156:             if os.path.isdir(s):
157:                 shutil.copytree(s, d)
158:             else:
159:                 shutil.copy2(s, d)
160:         except Exception as e:
161:             log(f"Warning: Failed to merge service file {item}: {e}")
162:     if os.path.exists(svc_dst):
163:         try_authenticode_sign(svc_dst)
164:         log("Added RemoteDesktopHostService.exe")
165:         return True
166:     log("ERROR: RemoteDesktopHostService.exe missing after merge.")
167:     return False

155: def _scaled_toplevel_geometry(self, newGeometry=None):
156:     if newGeometry is None:
157:         return _orig_toplevel_geometry(self)
158:     try:
159:         import re
160:         m = re.match(r'^(\d+)x(\d+)(?:\+([+-]?\d+)\+([+-]?\d+))?$', newGeometry)
161:         if m:
162:             w = int(m.group(1))
163:             h = int(m.group(2))
164:             x = m.group(3)
165:             y = m.group(4)
166:             scale = self.winfo_fpixels('1i') / 96.0
167:             sw = int(w * scale)
168:             sh = int(h * scale)
169:             if x is not None and y is not None:
170:                 adj_x = int(x) - (sw - w) // 2
171:                 adj_y = int(y) - (sh - h) // 2

173:             else:
174:                 newGeometry = f"{sw}x{sh}"
175:     except Exception as e:

+    def adjust_host_window_size(self):
+        """Tự động điều chỉnh kích thước cửa sổ Host vừa khít nội dung thực tế (không dư thừa khoảng trống)."""
+        try:
+            self.update_idletasks()
+            scale = self.winfo_fpixels('1i') / 96.0
+            req_w = self.winfo_reqwidth()
+            req_h = self.winfo_reqheight()
+            if req_h <= 100:
+                return
+            w = max(int(380 * scale), req_w)
+            h = req_h
+            self.minsize(w, h)
+            self.maxsize(w, h)
+
+            cur_geom = self.geometry()
+            pos = ""
+            if "+" in cur_geom:
+                pos = "+" + "+".join(cur_geom.split("+")[1:])
+            else:
+                screen_w = self.winfo_screenwidth()
+                screen_h = self.winfo_screenheight()
+                x = max(0, (screen_w - w) // 2)
+                y = max(0, (screen_h - h) // 2)
+                pos = f"+{x}+{y}"
+
+            new_geom = f"{w}x{h}{pos}"
+            self.geometry(new_geom)
+            self.last_normal_geometry = new_geom
+        except Exception as e:
+            print(f"[Host] adjust_host_window_size error: {e}")

1122: def _worker_screen_size():
1123:     try:
1124:         import mss
1125:         with mss.mss() as sct:
1126:             m = sct.monitors[0]
1127:             return int(m["width"]), int(m["height"]), dict(m)
1128:     except Exception:
1129:         pass
1130:     try:
1131:         import ctypes
1132:         w = int(ctypes.windll.user32.GetSystemMetrics(78)) or int(ctypes.windll.user32.GetSystemMetrics(0))
1133:         h = int(ctypes.windll.user32.GetSystemMetrics(79)) or int(ctypes.windll.user32.GetSystemMetrics(1))
1134:         left = int(ctypes.windll.user32.GetSystemMetrics(76))
1135:         top = int(ctypes.windll.user32.GetSystemMetrics(77))
1136:         if w < 1 or h < 1:
1137:             w, h = 1024, 768
1138:         return w, h, {"left": left, "top": top, "width": w, "height": h}
1139:     except Exception:
1140:         return 1024, 768, {"left": 0, "top": 0, "width": 1024, "height": 768}

1143: def _worker_local_ip():
1144:     try:
1145:         s = socket.socket(socket.AF_INET, socket.SOCK_DGRAM)
1146:         s.connect(("8.8.8.8", 80))
1147:         ip = s.getsockname()[0]
1148:         s.close()
1149:         return ip
1150:     except Exception:
1151:         return ""

1154: def _worker_desktop_name():
1155:     try:
1156:         import ctypes
1157:         h_desk = ctypes.windll.user32.GetThreadDesktop(ctypes.windll.kernel32.GetCurrentThreadId())
1158:         name = ctypes.create_unicode_buffer(256)
1159:         size = ctypes.c_ulong(256)
1160:         if ctypes.windll.user32.GetUserObjectInformationW(h_desk, 2, name, size, None):
1161:             return name.value.lower()
1162:     except Exception:
1163:         pass
1164:     return "default"
1165:

1167: def _worker_session_id():
1168:     try:
1169:         import ctypes
1170:         sid = ctypes.c_ulong()
1171:         if ctypes.windll.kernel32.ProcessIdToSessionId(ctypes.windll.kernel32.GetCurrentProcessId(), ctypes.byref(sid)):
1172:             return sid.value
1173:     except Exception:
1174:         pass
1175:     return 1

214: def merge_full_service(app_dist_dir):
215:     """Ghép RemoteDesktopService.exe + deps thiếu vào app.dist (giống slim merge của Host)."""
216:     service_dist_dir = os.path.join(workspace_dir, "dist_nuitka_service", "windows_service_loop.dist")
217:     if not os.path.exists(service_dist_dir):
218:         return False
219:     svc_src = os.path.join(service_dist_dir, "windows_service_loop.exe")
220:     svc_dst = os.path.join(app_dist_dir, "RemoteDesktopService.exe")
221:     if not os.path.exists(svc_src):
222:         log("ERROR: windows_service_loop.exe missing in service dist.")
223:         return False
224:     log("Merging RemoteDesktopService.exe (slim — only missing runtime files)...")
225:     shutil.copy2(svc_src, svc_dst)
226:     for item in os.listdir(service_dist_dir):
227:         if item.lower() == "windows_service_loop.exe":
228:             continue
229:         s = os.path.join(service_dist_dir, item)
230:         d = os.path.join(app_dist_dir, item)
231:         if os.path.exists(d):
232:             continue
233:         try:
234:             if os.path.isdir(s):
235:                 shutil.copytree(s, d)
236:             else:
237:                 shutil.copy2(s, d)
238:         except Exception as e:
239:             log(f"Warning: Failed to merge service file {item}: {e}")
240:     try_authenticode_sign(svc_dst)
241:     log("Added RemoteDesktopService.exe")
242:     return True
